To alleviate notation, we will also drop the ‘rel’ tag on the angle from now on.

6.1
Potential-only & Tails

The contribution from generic tail effects to the in-in effective action has been computed within the EFT approach in
[
60
]
, yielding

\displaystyle S_{I_{2}\rm(T)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}M_{+}}{5}\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega}{2\pi}\omega^{6}I_{-}^{ij}(-\omega)I_{+}^{ij}(\omega)\left[-\frac{1}{d-3}+\frac{41}{30}+i\pi\,\textrm{sign}(\omega)-\log\left(\frac{\omega^{2}e^{\gamma_{\textrm{E}}}}{\pi\mu^{2}}\right)\right]\,,

(6.4)

\displaystyle S_{I_{3}\rm(T)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}M_{+}}{189}\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega}{2\pi}\omega^{8}I_{-}^{ijk}(-\omega)I_{+}^{ijk}(\omega)\left[-\frac{1}{d-3}+\frac{82}{35}+i\pi\,\textrm{sign}(\omega)-\log\left(\frac{\omega^{2}e^{\gamma_{\textrm{E}}}}{\pi\mu^{2}}\right)\right]\,,

\displaystyle S_{J_{2}\rm(T)}

\displaystyle=\frac{32G^{2}M_{+}}{90}\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega}{2\pi}\omega^{6}J_{-}^{a|ij}(-\omega)J_{+}^{a|ij}(\omega)\left[-\frac{1}{d-3}+\frac{49}{20}+i\pi\,\textrm{sign}(\omega)-\log\left(\frac{\omega^{2}e^{\gamma_{\textrm{E}}}}{\pi\mu^{2}}\right)\right]\,,

for the terms that are relevant for our purposes here (see
[
49
,
60
,
59
]
for more details). As it is well known, the
1/(d-3)
pole and factor of
\log\mu
cancels out against the contribution from potential modes
[
49
,
55
,
54
,
66
]
to 5PN order, yielding finite and ambiguity-free results.

From the effective action we then obtain the equations and motion. For the conservative part, one can instead simply evaluate the conservative action (which follows ignoring the
i\pi\,\textrm{sign}(\omega)
and dividing by a factor of two
[
49
]
) and taking a derivative with respect to the angular momentum. This was performed in
[
64
]
using previous EFT results, obtaining

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{j\rm(pot+T)}^{(4,\nu^{2})\rm cons}

\displaystyle=-\frac{8919}{1400}\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}\,.

(6.5)

For the dissipative part, on the other hand, we must compute the impulse from the radiative force, involving the factors of
i\pi\,\textrm{sign}(\omega)
in (
6.4
). As it is well known, these term reproduce the total radiated energy due to tail effects
[
49
,
64
]
. Furthermore, their contribution(s) to the (relative) scattering angle may be also obtained by using linear-response theory. Following the analysis in
[
64
]
, we find

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{j\rm(T)}^{(4)\rm diss}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6.6)

The vanishing of the dissipative part of the tail term at
{\cal O}(G^{4})
turns out to have important consequences regarding the origin of various different contributions to the near-zone dynamics.

6.2
Failed tail, Memories & RR
2

We start with the failed-tail and memory parts, which follows via (
6.1
)
evaluated on the deflected trajectories,

{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)={\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}(t)+\delta_{\textrm{(FT)}}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)+\delta_{\textrm{(M)}}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)\,,

(6.7)

where
{\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}={\boldsymbol{b}}+{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\infty}t
, and the perturbed trajectories,
\delta_{\textrm{(FT)}}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)
and
\delta_{\textrm{(M)}}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)
, satisfy

\delta_{(\textrm{X})}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{r}}}(t)={\boldsymbol{a}}_{\textrm{(X)}}(t)\,,\qquad\textrm{X}\in\{\textrm{FT, M}\}\,.

(6.8)

Since the additional nonlocal-in-time memory term does not contribute to the total impulse, we may ignore it in what follows.

We find, for each, two contributions to the total impulse. Firstly, we have the hereditary radiation-reaction force evaluated up to the Newtonian deflection,

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{FT})}=M\nu\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\textrm{(FT)}}\Big|_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}=-\frac{11}{16}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,,

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{M})}=M\nu\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\textrm{(M)}}\Big|_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}=\frac{871}{2240}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,.

(6.9)

The second part is obtained by including
\delta_{\textrm{(X)}}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)
in the Newtonian acceleration,

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{FT})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{N})}=M\nu\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\textrm{(N)}}\Big|_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{FT})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}=-\frac{35}{16}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,,

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{M})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{N})}=M\nu\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\textrm{(N)}}\Big|_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{M})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}=\frac{7593}{2240}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,.

(6.10)

Combing all contributions we arrive at

\displaystyle\Delta^{\textrm{(N)}}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{FT})}+\Delta^{(\textrm{FT})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{N})}=-\frac{23}{8}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,,

(6.11)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{M})}+\Delta^{(\textrm{M})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{N})}=\frac{529}{140}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,.

(6.12)

For the remaining contribution at second order in the radiation-reaction force, we perform the same steps, but in this case we must evaluate (
6.1
) on trajectories obeying an equation as in (
6.8
) with
X\in\rm\{N,RR,RR^{2}\}
terms given by the accelerations in (
4.26
) and (
4.27
).
10
10

10

Let us remind the reader that, even though
{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\rm(RR^{2})}
is a Schott-type term at
{\cal O}(G^{3})
, it enters in the impulse at
{\cal O}(G^{4})
through the deflected trajectory on the Newtonian force.

Using the same notation as before we find the following intermediate contributions,

\Delta^{\rm(N)}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{\rm(RR^{2})}=M\nu\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\rm(RR^{2})}\Big|_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}=\frac{3567}{200}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,,

(6.13)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\rm(RR)}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{\rm(RR)}

\displaystyle=M\nu\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\rm(RR)}\Big|_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{RR})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}=-\frac{479}{25}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,,

(6.14)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{N})}

\displaystyle=M\nu\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\textrm{(N)}}\Big|_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{0}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{N})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{RR})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)+\delta_{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}=-\frac{791}{40}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,,

(6.15)

which combines into

\Delta^{\rm(N)}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{\rm(RR^{2})}+\Delta^{\rm(RR)}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{\rm(RR)}+\Delta^{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{(\textrm{N})}=-\frac{211}{10}\pi\frac{G^{4}M^{5}\nu^{3}}{b^{4}}v_{\infty}^{3}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+{\cal O}(G^{5})\,.

(6.16)

Although we disagree with the previous hereditary (failed-tail and memory) values computed in
[
67
]
, the expression in (
6.16
) is consistent with their combined result for radiation-reaction-square terms.

6.3
Total deflection angle

Adding up the results from nonlinear radiation-reaction effects we obtain the total impulse,
from which we can derive the scattering angle, via

\chi=\arccos\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{-}\cdot{\boldsymbol{p}}_{+}}{|{\boldsymbol{p}}_{-}||{\boldsymbol{p}}_{+}|}\right)\,,

(6.17)

with the
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{\pm}
the incoming and outgoing relative momenta, respectively. Using the standard convention that the deflection is positive along the
-\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}
direction (as the leading order), the individual contributions coming from failed-tail, memory and (combined) radiation-reaction-square effects, are given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{(4,\nu^{2})}_{j(\rm FT)}

\displaystyle=\frac{23}{16}\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}\,,

(6.18)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{(4,\nu^{2})}_{j(\rm M)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{529}{280}\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{(4,\nu^{2})}_{j({\rm RR}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{211}{20}\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}\,,

respectively. Summing these results together with the total potential and tail terms in (
6.5
) (and
(
6.6
)), we finally arrive at

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{(4,\nu^{2})\rm tot}_{j(\rm even)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1491}{400}\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}\,,

(6.19)

for the even-in-velocity
{\cal O}(G^{4})
coefficient of the total (relative) scattering angle, entering at second order in the mass ratio. This result is in perfect agreement in the overlap with the value computed in
[
28
]
at 4PM order.

6.4
Conservative part

Using the expressions in (
5.8
), (
5.11
) and (
5.15
) for the hereditary and radiation-reaction-square contributions to the effective action, respectively, we can readily derive the associated correction to the conservative deflection angle at
{\cal O}(G^{4})
, by evaluating the (radial) action on the Newtonian trajectory to the given order in
G
, and taking a derivative with respect to the angular momentum (see App.
C
). We find

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{(4,\nu^{2})\rm cons}_{j(\rm FT)}

\displaystyle=\frac{69}{80}\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}\,,

(6.20)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{(4,\nu^{2})\rm cons}_{j(\rm M)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{477}{560}\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}^{(4,\nu^{2})\rm cons}_{j({\rm RR}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{159}{25}\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}\,,

which, together with (
6.5
), yields

\tilde{\chi}^{(4,\nu^{2})\rm cons}_{j\rm{(pot+T+FT+M+RR^{2})}}=\bigg(-\frac{8919}{1400}+\frac{69}{80}-\frac{477}{560}+\frac{159}{25}\bigg)\pi\nu^{2}v_{\infty}^{6}=0\,,

(6.21)

in perfect agreement in the overlap with the conservative 4PM result in
[
24
,
40
]
, as well as the value inferred from the TF formalism in
[
64
]
, consistently with the expected (polynomial) mass-scaling of the scattering computation
[
92
]
(see also
[
93
,
18
]
).

7
Conclusions & Outlook

In this paper we have computed the missing hereditary effects in the near-zone two-body (relative) dynamics, yielding the following result for the complete (in-in) effective action to 5PN order,

\displaystyle

\displaystyle S^{\rm tot}_{\rm 5PN}=S_{\rm(pot)}+S_{\rm(RR)}+S_{\rm(T)}-\frac{G^{2}}{15}\int\!{\mathrm{d}}t\,L_{+}^{kl}I^{(4)}_{-,kj}I^{(3)}_{+,jl}+\frac{G^{2}}{30}\int\!{\mathrm{d}}t\,L_{-}^{kl}I^{(4)}_{+,kj}I^{(3)}_{+,jl}

(7.1)

\displaystyle+\frac{G^{2}}{5}\int{\mathrm{d}}t\left(\frac{1}{2}I_{-,ij}I^{(4)}_{+,jk}I^{(4)}_{+,ki}-I^{(4)}_{-,ij}I^{(4)}_{+,jk}I_{+,ki}+\frac{1}{7}I^{(2)}_{-,ij}I^{(3)}_{+,jk}I^{(3)}_{+,ki}+\frac{2}{7}I^{(3)}_{-,ij}I^{(3)}_{+,jk}I^{(2)}_{+,ki}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{2G^{2}}{35}\int\!\!{\mathrm{d}}t\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\left\{I_{-}^{(2)ij}(t)\int{\mathrm{d}}\tau\vartheta(t-\tau)I_{+}^{(3)jk}(\tau)I_{+}^{(3)ki}(\tau)\right\}\,,

where
S_{\rm(pot)}
are the potential-only contributions obtained in
[
66
]
, and the linear radiation-reaction,
S_{\rm(RR)}
, and tail terms,
S_{\rm(T)}
, are given in (
4.21
) and (
6.4
), respectively.

Our results differ from previous derivations in several notable ways. First of all, there is a crucial term (depending on
L^{ij}_{-}
) in the failed-tail contribution which was not included before. Secondly, due to the enforcing of diffemorphism invariance in the Feynman rules, the coefficients of the (local-in-time) memory contributions also differ with the values in
[
61
,
67
]
. Finally, because of the nontrivial soft-frequency limit of the relevant Feynman integrals, we have uncovered a novel nonlocal-in-time (boundary) term. From the expression in (
7.1
) we derived the equations of motions, and demonstrated the consistency between the near- and far-zone GW fluxes, paying particular attention to the connection between boundary terms and the flux of (canonical) angular momentum. From the equations of motion we computed the contribution to the total (even-in-velocity) relative scattering angle at
{\cal O}(G^{4})
, including the potential-only, tail, and radiation-reaction-square terms, finding complete agreement in the overlap with the state of the art in the PM expansion
[
28
]
.

We have also discussed the split into conservative and dissipative parts, following Feynman’s prescription. We have found several subtleties due to the introduction of
\mathscr{P}
-integrals. Moreover, we demonstrated the presence of radiation-reaction-square terms in the conservative sector. After adding all the relevant pieces, the conservative dynamics at 5PN/4PM may be obtained from the following effective action,

\displaystyle S^{\rm cons}_{\rm 5PN/4PM}

\displaystyle=S_{\rm(pot)}+S^{\rm cons}_{\rm(T)}+\frac{G^{2}}{5}\int{\mathrm{d}}t\bigg\{-\frac{1}{6}\,L^{kl}I^{(4)ki}I^{(3)li}+

(7.2)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{7}I^{(2)ij}I^{(3)jk}I^{(3)ik}-\frac{1}{2}I^{ij}I^{(4)jk}I^{(4)ik}+

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5}\left(-L^{ki}I^{(4)kj}I^{(3)ij}+Q^{ki}I^{kj(4)}I^{(4)ij}+Q^{(2)ki}I^{(3)kj}I^{(3)ij}\right)\bigg\}\,,

expanded to
{\cal O}(G^{4})
, where
S^{\rm cons}_{\rm(T)}
are the conservative parts of tail terms
[
49
,
60
]
, and the above expression incorporates the remaining failed-tail, memory, and radiation-reaction-square effects, respectively. Notice that the last line introduces terms that resemble the others, as well as dependence on traces that are absent from hereditary effects. From here we derived the conservative scattering angle, finding also perfect agreement with the PN-exact
{\cal O}(G^{4})
value obtained in
[
24
,
40
]
, as well as the 5PN/4PM result inferred from the TF approach
[
64
]
.

As it was discussed in
[
32
]
, upon subtracting nonlocal-in-time tail effects (from the
\log\omega^{2}
in (
6.4
)), the resulting scattering angle may be analytically continued through the boundary-to-bound dictionary
[
18
,
19
,
25
]
to compute observables for generic bound orbits, or incorporated into a local-in-time Hamiltonian (see also
[
94
]
). Likewise, at higher orders in
G
, the expression in (
7.2
) will incorporate all of the “tail-like” 5PN contributions to the conservative sector, provided the multipole moments with
n\leq 2
derivatives are kept unperturbed (setting the acceleration to zero). However, starting at 5PM, other conservative (Feynman) memory effects—depending on an integral over the principal value—will introduce nonlocal-in-time terms that are not captured by (
7.2
). We will return to a more in-depth discussion of the conservative sector and the connection to scattering elsewhere.

There are various other aspects of our calculation that have uncovered somewhat unexpected issues that deserve further study:

•

Tails vs Memories

As we discovered, the 4PM dissipative contribution, denoted as
\chi_{b,\rm rel}^{(4)\rm 2rad}
in
[
28
]
, is entirely captured by failed-tail, memory, and radiation-reaction-square terms in the PN EFT approach. Indeed, the overall scaling with the mass already implied that this term had to originate from the product of three quadrupole moments (albeit one of them is
static
, as in tail-like interactions).
11
11

11

This is consistent with the fact that
\chi_{b,\rm rel}^{(4)\rm 2rad}
can be obtained by combining the tail-induced radiated linear momentum (see e.g. Eq. (H1) in
[
64
]
) together with the relations uncovered in Eqs. (12.34-12.36) of
[
95
]
.
The situation gets more interesting at higher orders in
G
. Starting at 5PM, regions with
three
radiation modes will contribute in relativistic scattering computations (such as tail-of-tail effects). Yet, after IBP reduction we find that (modulo soft-frequency limits) all of the leading memory effects reduce to master integrals with
two
radiation modes (not necessarily with the same frequency). This apparent dichotomy is remediated by noticing that the time derivatives in hereditary terms can themselves be evaluated on the (linear) radiation-reaction force. Moreover, the three-bubble diagram (as well as the tail of the memory) will also enter at
{\cal O}(G^{5})
, evaluated on the Newtonian solution. These considerations illustrate the intricate connections between the multipole expansion, the method of regions, and the various contributions from nonlinear interactions in both PM and PN effective theories.

•

Conservative-like RR
2

Conservative effects entailed also terms at second order in the linear radiation-reaction force. At first sight, the Burke-Thorne force is purely dissipative, which is manifest in the fact that Feynman’s computation is purely imaginary, and so is the associated radiation-reacted trajectory. The conservative part of the force arises after incorporating the radiation-reaction acceleration onto itself. As in the case of electromagnetism, this was sufficient at
{\cal O}(G^{4})
. However, at higher orders, conservative-like contributions to the impulse may arise from iterations over the radiation-reacted trajectory itself. Yet, this cannot be inferred from the iteration over a real conservative force, which implies that one has to be careful when splitting conservative and dissipative radiation-reaction-square terms. In particular, to avoid double counting, not only we ought to isolate the conservative part of
{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\rm(RR^{2})}
, but in principle also the iterations over
{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\rm(RR)}
.

•

Conservative (time) nonlocality

As we have shown, despite Feynman’s prescription introducing new non-analyticities in the frequencies, the latter do not play a role in the derivation of conservative effects at
{\cal O}(G^{4})
, which may be obtained directly from the effective action in (
7.2
), having only the already known time nonlocality due to tail effects
[
49
]
. However, starting at
{\cal O}(G^{5})
Feynman’s prescription may incur in additional nonlocal-in-time (memory and radiation-reaction-square) effects, proportional to a
\mathscr{P}
-integral. Yet, since the total result derived from (
7.1
) is local, any extra nonlocality will cancel out against a counterpart in the dissipative sector. This suggests that we can perform a splitting of the (relative) dynamics into local-in-time conservative and dissipative terms (for instance by constructing time-symmetric expressions in terms of retarded and advanced propagators). However, we cannot assume the former will always obey the same (polynomial) mass scaling inherited from Feynman’s computation, as in (
6.21
). This implies that we may not be able to simply compare PN and PM results beyond 4PM order, and only total values may be immune to different choices.

•

Soft-frequency limit

Another subtle issue is the soft-frequency limit of the Feynman integral involving the cubic coupling. Even though it does not affect the impulse, it does contribute to the flux of (canonical) angular momentum. This is not entirely surprising, since it is well known that the angular-momentum flux is sensitive to the waveform in the
\omega\to 0
limit, see e.g.
[
96
,
97
,
98
,
99
]
. Moreover, we found that the soft-frequency contribution due to memory effects in (
3.35
) starts at
{\cal O}(G^{5})
, which is also consistent with the results in
[
89
,
100
]
. However, there are two key aspects regarding the derivation of the near-zone action. Firstly, the need to avoid enforcing the energy/frequency conservation prior to performing the IBP decomposition; and secondly, the appearance of factors of
\tfrac{\omega^{3}}{\omega+i0}
which cannot be naively simplified in the presence of a nontrivial analytic structure of the multipole moments. The soft-frequency-limit contribution enters as a boundary term, thus affecting the canonical momentum while the evolution equations for the position and velocities remain unaltered. This also suggests that the impact of the nonlocal-in-time angular-momentum flux on the evolution of the GW phase is more subtle than what we would have naively expected from the far-zone computation.

In addition to the above there are other directions worth of further exploration. As we discussed, we have concentrated on the relative near-zone dynamics.
12
12

12

As we mentioned, the center-of-mass recoil can be included through terms depending on the center-of-mass position and velocity in the effective action, yielding a correction to the radiation-reaction force, or directly via the flux of momentum using the Ward identity.
The issue with the relative part, for instance for the impulse, is that it does not capture (directly) the total radiated momentum
[
64
]
. The latter, however, is the only type of GW flux from failed-tail, memory, and radiation-reaction-square effects that does not turn into a total time derivative after writing the result in terms of positions and velocities (modulo the nonlocal-in-time flux of canonical angular momentum). Hence, these effects are
conservative
from the point of view of the relative dynamics, since they may be reabsorbed into the left-hand-side of a balance-type equation. This implies, for instance, that the total (even-in-velocity) relative scattering angle due to nonlinear gravitational forces at 5PN order may be described in terms of a Hamiltonian, albeit without the mass-polynomiality of the Feynman result, as we see already in (
6.19
). We will return to this issue in more detail in forthcoming work.

Finally, there is the connection with the more traditional Multipolar-Post-Minkowskian (MPM) formalism
[
101
]
. Although we have checked that the total radiated energy and angular-momentum agree with the MPM results in
[
95
]
,
13
13

13

This follows form the fact that the (in-in) EFT and MPM fluxes agree up to Schott terms.
the explicit functional form of the memory part of the
h_{ij}^{\rm TT}
one-point function—written in terms of products of derivatives of the quadrupole moment—does not formally agree with the MPM form
[
80
]
, except for the nonlocal-in-time contribution. Given the notorious differences between both frameworks, this is somewhat expected. For starters, the couplings in the effective action do not include traces, while these are kept throughout the MPM approach. Furthermore, the EFT multipoles are defined with respect to a locally-flat frame and matched with the (pseudo-)stress-energy tensor; whereas in the MPM formalism other coordinates and matching conditions are used.
Yet, agreement has been found so far in all observable quantities, notably the recent rederivation in
[
33
]
of the energy flux and radiated power at 3PN order, first obtained in the MPM approach
[
9
]
; as well as the rederivation in
[
52
]
of 4PN conservative tail effects, first obtained within the EFT approach
[
49
]
. Moreover, in the PM regime, the results in
[
95
]
are consistent with the total values in
[
28
]
, and recent MPM and amplitude-based approaches have produced matching results for the waveform in the overlapping realm of validity
[
102
,
103
,
104
]
. Hence, we expect that once all the pieces are collected (including terms at second order in the radiation-reaction, which are also formally different), the value of the full waveform in the EFT and MPM approaches, once written in terms of gauge-invariant quantities, will ultimately agree. We will return to the explicit comparison elsewhere. At the same time, we expect the MPM derivation of the 5PN near-zone dynamics will agree as well with the results reported here, which may help us elucidate the connection between formalisms.
14
14

14

Another possible route is to recast the EFT derivations in a way that resembles the MPM approach
[
105
]
.

Appendix A
Background gauge & Ward identities

Throughout this paper we have emphasized the importance of the background-gauge fixing in (
2.9
) for diffeomorphism invariance and the validity of the Ward identities. However, for the computations we performed, the additional part of the Feynman rule due to the background-field gauge did not play a role. Needless to say, this will not be true in general. We show here an example where the background-field method is essential to guarantee that the associated
T^{\mu\nu}(x)
satisfies the Ward identity.

Let us consider the diagrams in Fig.
4
responsible for the tail effects, where we included also the contributions which are needed for a nonstatic monopole term. Hence, we have a (Bondi) mass/energy coupling where the
M(\omega_{1})
is
not
proportional to
\delta(\omega_{1})
and include, for instance, corrections due to GW emission. In background gauge we have the gauge-fixing harmonic term

S_{\textrm{GF}}=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{4}x\sqrt{-\bar{g}}\bar{g}^{\mu\nu}\left[\bar{g}^{\alpha\beta}\bar{\nabla}_{\alpha}h_{\beta\mu}-\frac{\bar{g}^{\alpha\beta}}{2}\bar{\nabla}_{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}\right]\left[\bar{g}^{\rho\sigma}\bar{\nabla}_{\rho}h_{\sigma\nu}-\frac{\bar{g}^{\rho\sigma}}{2}\bar{\nabla}_{\nu}h_{\rho\sigma}\right]\,,

(A.1)

which, after expanding the background metric (with
\xi
just a placeholder)

\bar{g}_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+\xi\kappa\bar{h}_{\mu\nu}\,,

(A.2)

implies (schematically)

\displaystyle S_{\textrm{GF}}=\int{\mathrm{d}}^{4}x\bigg\{(\partial h)^{2}+\kappa\xi\bigg[\bar{h}(\partial h)^{2}+(\partial\bar{h})^{2}h+(\partial\bar{h})(\partial h)h\bigg]+\cdots\bigg\}\,,

(A.3)

modifying the cubic interaction for the (“quantum”)
h_{\mu\nu}
field and the background
\bar{h}_{\mu\nu}
. Returning to the diagrams in Fig.
4
, we find that Fig.
4
(c) is manifestly zero, since it is always proportional to a scaleless integral which in dim. reg. vanishes. On the other hand, both Fig.
4
(a) and Fig.
4
(b) contribute and, after an IBP reduction, the result can be written as

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}_{+,\ref{Fig:Wtail}\textrm{(a)+(b)}}(k)

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega_{1}}{2\pi}M_{+}(\omega)I_{+}^{\alpha\beta}(\omega-\omega_{1})\Big[\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}},\xi)\mathcal{I}_{1,0}

(A.4)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}},\xi)\mathcal{I}_{0,1}+\mathcal{N}^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}},\xi)\mathcal{I}_{1,1}\Big]\,,

where we encounter the same master integrals we had before, i.e.

\mathcal{I}_{ab}\equiv\int_{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\frac{1}{\big[(\omega_{1}+i0^{+})^{2}-{\boldsymbol{q}}^{2}\big]^{a}\big[(\omega-\omega_{1}+i0^{+})^{2}-({\boldsymbol{k}}-{\boldsymbol{q}})^{2}\big]^{b}}\,.

(A.5)

Notice that all three tensorial structures
\mathcal{N}^{(n)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}},\xi)
depend on the gauge-fixing choice in (
A.1
) through the
\xi
parameter. In order to verify the Ward identity we then compute

\displaystyle k_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}_{+,{\rm Fig}.\ref{Fig:Wtail}\textrm{(a)(b)}}(k)

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega_{1}}{2\pi}M_{+}(\omega)I_{+}^{\alpha\beta}(\omega-\omega_{1})\Big[k_{\nu}\mathcal{N}^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}},\xi)\mathcal{I}_{1,0}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt+k_{\nu}\mathcal{N}^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}},\xi)\mathcal{I}_{0,1}+k_{\nu}\mathcal{N}^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu\nu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}},\xi)\mathcal{I}_{1,1}\Big]

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}(1-\xi)\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega_{1}}{2\pi}\omega_{1}M_{+}(\omega_{1})I_{+}^{\alpha\beta}(\omega-\omega_{1})\Big[\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}})\mathcal{I}_{1,0}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt+\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{(2)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}})\mathcal{I}_{0,1}+\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{(3)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}})\mathcal{I}_{1,1}\Big]\,,

(A.6)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta}{}^{\mu}(\omega,\omega_{1},{\boldsymbol{k}})
is the resulting tensorial structure after contracting with
k^{\nu}
, and we have factored out the
\xi
dependence already. First of all, we immediately notice that the Ward identity is automatically obeyed when
\dot{M}=0
, for which
\omega M(\omega)\propto\omega\delta(\omega)\to 0
. Secondly, for a nonstatic mass/energy coupling, the expression in (
A.6
) does not vanish unless we choose
\xi=1
. Namely, the background gauge-fixing action in (
A.1
). Finally, let us stress that in this scenario the diagram in Fig.
4
(b) is crucial to guarantee the validity of the Ward identity.

Figure 4
:
The Feynman diagrams needed for the computation of the tail contribution to
T_{+}^{\mu\nu}
. For simplicity we denote
k^{0}=\omega
and
q^{0}=\omega_{1}
.

Appendix B
Near-zone (local-in-time) GW fluxes

From the in-in computation, we will generally find the following form of the ‘dissipative’ part of the in-in effective action,

R_{\pm}=\sum_{\mathscr{M}}\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}\mathscr{F}_{+}^{L}(\mathscr{M_{+}})\,,

(B.1)

where
\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}
is a generic multipole,
\mathscr{F}_{+}^{L}(\mathscr{M_{+}})
is a function of the
\mathscr{M_{+}}
multipoles, both contracted over
L
indices. From here we obtain the energy and angular momentum losses from the near-zone dynamics (omitting the sum over particles for simplicity)

\displaystyle\dot{M}

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\bigg[\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{-}}\mathscr{F}_{+}^{L}(\mathscr{M_{+}})-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\Bigg(\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{v}}_{-}}\mathscr{F}_{+}^{L}(\mathscr{M_{+}})\Bigg)\bigg]\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}\,,

(B.2)

\displaystyle\dot{L}_{k}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{kij}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\bigg[\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}^{j}_{-}}\mathscr{F}_{+}^{L}(\mathscr{M_{+}})-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\Bigg(\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{v}}^{j}_{-}}\mathscr{F}_{+}^{L}(\mathscr{M_{+}})\Bigg)\bigg]\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}\,.

(B.3)

Starting with (
B.2
), and upon time averaging over an orbit, we have
[
76
]

\langle\dot{M}\rangle=\left\langle\bigg[{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{-}}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{v}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\cdot\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{v}}_{-}}\bigg]\mathscr{F}_{+}^{L}(\mathscr{M_{+}})\right\rangle\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}\,.

(B.4)

In order to evaluate the time derivatives, we use that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\ell}_{-}}\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{k}}{\partial x^{\ell}_{-}}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{k}}+\frac{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}^{k}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\ell}_{-}}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}^{k}}\bigg)\Big(\mathscr{M}_{1}^{L}-\mathscr{M}_{2}^{L}\Big)\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\delta^{k}{}_{\ell}}{2}\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{1}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{k}}-\frac{(-\delta^{k}{}_{\ell})}{2}\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{2}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}^{k}}\bigg)\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}=\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\ell}}\,,

(B.5)

and similarly for the derivative with respect to the velocity. Hence,
since the multipole moments do not depend explicitly on time (for a binary in isolation), we have

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathscr{M}^{L}({\boldsymbol{x}}(t),{\boldsymbol{v}}(t))={\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{v}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\cdot\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{v}}}\,,

(B.6)

such that

\langle\dot{M}\rangle=\left\langle\bigg[{\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{v}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\cdot\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{v}}}\bigg]\mathscr{F}^{L}(\mathscr{M})\right\rangle=\left\langle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\mathscr{M}^{L}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathscr{F}^{L}(\mathscr{M})\right\rangle\,.

(B.7)

The computation of the angular-momentum flux is a bit trickier. Let us restrict the manipulations here local-in-time effects. We use the expression in (
B.3
) and, upon time-averaging, we get

\langle\dot{{\boldsymbol{L}}}_{k}\rangle=\varepsilon_{kij}\bigg\langle\bigg[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}^{j}_{-}}+{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\frac{\partial\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{v}}^{j}_{-}}\bigg]\mathscr{F}_{+}^{L}(\mathscr{M_{+}})\bigg\rangle\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}\,.

(B.8)

In what follows we particularize for our case at hand, namely
\mathscr{M}_{-}^{L}=\{L_{-}^{ij},I_{-}^{ij}\}
. Without loss of generality, the angular momentum and quadrupole may be decomposed as

\displaystyle L_{-}^{ij}

\displaystyle=2f_{1}\big({\boldsymbol{x}}_{-}^{[i}{\boldsymbol{v}}_{+}^{j]}+{\boldsymbol{x}}_{+}^{[i}{\boldsymbol{v}}_{-}^{j]}\big)

(B.9)

\displaystyle I_{-}^{ij}

\displaystyle=\left(f_{2}\,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{-}^{(i}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{+}^{j)}+f_{3}\,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{-}^{(i}{\boldsymbol{v}}_{+}^{j)}+f_{4}\,{\boldsymbol{v}}_{-}^{(i}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{+}^{j)}+f_{5}\,{\boldsymbol{v}}_{-}^{(i}{\boldsymbol{v}}_{+}^{j)}\right)-{\rm traces}\,,

(B.10)

where the
f_{n}
’s,
n=1\cdots 5
, are only functions of the
+
variables (with other contributions annihilated by the PL). For instance, at leading order we have

I_{-}^{ij}=\sum_{a}m_{a}\bigg(2{\boldsymbol{x}}_{a,-}^{(i}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{a,+}^{j)}-\frac{2}{3}\delta^{ij}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{a,-}\cdot{\boldsymbol{x}}_{a,+}\bigg)\,.

(B.11)

From the general form in the above expressions we find

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\frac{\partial L_{-}^{ab}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}^{j}_{-}}+{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\frac{\partial L_{-}^{ab}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{v}}^{j}_{-}}\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}

\displaystyle=f_{1}\big({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{b}\delta_{j}{}^{a}+{\boldsymbol{x}}^{a}{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\delta_{j}{}^{b}-(a\leftrightarrow b)\big)=L_{i}{}^{b}\delta_{j}{}^{a}-L_{i}{}^{a}\delta_{j}{}^{b}\,

(B.12)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\frac{\partial I_{-}^{ab}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}^{j}_{-}}+{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\frac{\partial I_{-}^{ab}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{v}}^{j}_{-}}\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}

\displaystyle=2{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\big(f_{2}\,\delta_{j}{}^{(a}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{b)}+f_{3}\,\delta_{j}{}^{(a}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{b)}\big)+2{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\big(f_{4}\,\delta_{j}{}^{(a}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{b)}+f_{5}\,\delta_{j}{}^{(a}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{b)}\big)

\displaystyle=2I_{i}{}^{(a}\delta_{j}{}^{b)}+O\big(\delta_{i}{}^{(a}\delta_{j}{}^{b)}\big)\,,

(B.13)

where the last term represent the traces (that ultimately cancel out once contracted with antisymmetric terms). From here we arrive at the general structure for the flux of angular momentum

\displaystyle\langle\dot{{\boldsymbol{L}}}_{k}\rangle=\varepsilon_{kij}\left\langle I_{i\ell}\Big(\mathscr{F}_{\ell j}(\mathscr{M})+\mathscr{F}_{j\ell}(\mathscr{M})\Big)+L_{i\ell}\Big(\mathscr{F}_{j\ell}(\mathscr{M})-\mathscr{F}_{\ell j}(\mathscr{M})\Big)\right\rangle\,.

(B.14)

Appendix C
Abraham-Lorentz conservative effects

The conservative relativistic scattering of two (nonspinning) charges in electrodynamics due to radiative effects was obtained in
[
82
]
, at fourth order in the coupling (see ancillary file in
[
82
]
). Since (classical) electromagnetism is inherently a linear theory, the existence of such conservative-like terms can only be associated with contributions at second-order in the Abraham-(Dirac)-Lorentz force. We reproduce here this effect, at leading order in the PN expansion, from the point of view of the EFT approach
[
14
,
71
]
.

At leading order in the multipole expansion, and ignoring the gravitational field, the long-distance effective action for the two-body system takes the form

S_{\rm eff}=-\frac{1}{4}\int{\mathrm{d}}^{4}xF_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-\sqrt{4\pi\alpha}(Q_{1}+Q_{2})\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,A^{\mu}V_{\mu}+\sqrt{4\pi\alpha}\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{d}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{E}}+\cdots\,,

(C.1)

\displaystyle\alpha\equiv e^{2}/4\pi\,,\qquad F_{\mu\nu}=2\partial_{[\mu}A_{\nu]}\,,\qquad E_{i}=-F_{0i}

(C.2)

\displaystyle\quad V^{\mu}=\delta^{\mu}_{0}\,,\qquad{\boldsymbol{d}}(t)=Q_{1}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}(t)+Q_{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2}(t)\,,

in the center-of-mass frame. We have only kept the coupling to the dipole,
{\boldsymbol{d}}
, which is the relevant term at leading PN order. In what follows we denote as
q_{a}\equiv Q_{a}/m_{a}
(
a=1,2
) the charge/mass ratio.

In-in
computation

We follow the same steps as in the gravitational case, yielding

\displaystyle iS[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\pm}]=i(4\pi\alpha)\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega}{2\pi}\int_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\bigg[k_{i}k_{j}d^{i}_{-}(-\omega)d_{+}^{j}(\omega)-\omega^{2}d^{i}_{-}(-\omega)d_{+}^{i}(\omega)\bigg]\frac{1}{(\omega+i0^{+})^{2}-{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\,,

(C.3)

which reduces to

\displaystyle S[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\pm}]

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha}{3}\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega}{2\pi}(i\omega^{3})d^{i}_{-}(-\omega)d_{+}^{i}(\omega)=\frac{2\alpha}{3}\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(t)\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}^{(3)}_{+}(t)\,.

(C.4)

From the in-in action we get the radiation-reaction (Abraham-Lorentz) acceleration for each particle

{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a\rm(RR)}=\frac{2\alpha}{3m_{a}}\frac{\partial{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(t)}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{a,-}(t)}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}^{(3)}_{+}(t)\bigg|_{\textrm{PL}}=\frac{2\alpha}{3}q_{a}{\boldsymbol{d}}^{(3)}(t)=\frac{2\alpha}{3}q_{a}\big(m_{1}q_{1}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}+m_{2}q_{2}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}\big)\,.

(C.5)

In order to obtain the leading order contribution, we replace the acceleration on the right-hand side of (
C.5
) by the Coulomb force, yielding the relative acceleration
15
15

15

Notice that it vanishes when
q_{1}=q_{2}
.

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\rm(RR)}

\displaystyle\equiv{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1\rm(RR)}-{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2\rm(RR)}=\frac{2}{3}\alpha^{2}M\nu\,q_{1}q_{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}{r^{3}(t)}\right)\,.

(C.6a)

Since it is a total derivative, the contribution from this acceleration to the total impulse is clearly zero, regardless of the trajectory. That means that the only correction comes from the radiation-reaction deflected trajectory (or ‘iteration’
[
20
]
)
{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)={\boldsymbol{b}}+{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\infty}t+\delta^{\rm(RR)}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)
, which solves the equation

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}}\delta^{(\textrm{RR})}{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)={\boldsymbol{a}}^{(\textrm{RR})}\,,

(C.7)

into the Coulomb force, yielding

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{RR})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{3}M^{4}}{b^{3}}\frac{q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}\nu}{3v_{\infty}^{2}}\bigg[-4\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}-\pi\frac{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\infty}}{v_{\infty}}\bigg]\,,

(C.8a)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{RR})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{3}M^{4}}{b^{3}}\frac{q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}\nu}{3v_{\infty}^{2}}\bigg[4\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}+\pi\frac{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\infty}}{v_{\infty}}\bigg]\,,

(C.8b)

where, as in the main text,
({\boldsymbol{b}},{\boldsymbol{v}}_{\infty})
are the impact parameter and relative velocity at infinity, respectively. This agrees with the nonrelativistic expansion of the result in
[
27
]
.

The acceleration at second order in the radiation-reaction (RR
2
) can be obtained by plugging back the radiation-reaction acceleration onto the right-hand side of the force,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a\rm(RR^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{9}\alpha^{2}q_{a}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\bigg[m_{1}q_{1}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}+m_{2}q_{2}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}\bigg]\,

(C.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{9}\alpha^{3}M^{2}\nu q_{a}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)q_{1}q_{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}}\frac{{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}{r^{3}(t)}\,,

where in the last equality we inputed the Coulomb acceleration. Upon evaluating the time derivatives, we find

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a\rm(RR^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{9}\alpha^{3}M^{2}\nu q_{a}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)q_{1}q_{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})

(C.10)

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\bigg(15\frac{({\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{r}})^{2}}{r^{7}}-3\frac{v^{2}}{r^{5}}\bigg){\boldsymbol{r}}-6\frac{({\boldsymbol{r}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{v}})}{r^{5}}{\boldsymbol{v}}-2\alpha mq_{1}q_{2}\frac{{\boldsymbol{r}}}{r^{6}}\bigg]\,.

such that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\rm(RR^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{9}\alpha^{3}M^{2}\nu\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)q_{1}q_{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}

(C.11)

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\bigg(15\frac{({\boldsymbol{v}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{r}})^{2}}{r^{7}}-3\frac{v^{2}}{r^{5}}\bigg){\boldsymbol{r}}-6\frac{({\boldsymbol{r}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{v}})}{r^{5}}{\boldsymbol{v}}-2\alpha mq_{1}q_{2}\frac{{\boldsymbol{r}}}{r^{6}}\bigg]\,,

for the relative acceleration, where the
\alpha^{4}
term arises from the inclusion, once again, of the Coulomb force.
Because of the structure of the radiation-reaction-square force, it is straightforward to show that
16
16

16

Notice that in order to obtain this result from the expression in (
C.11
) we must also take into account not only straight motion but also the leading order deflection due to the Coulomb field.

\int{\mathrm{d}}t{\boldsymbol{a}}_{a}^{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}\sim\bigg[\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\frac{{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}{r^{3}(t)}\bigg]\bigg|^{\infty}_{-\infty}=0\,.

(C.12)

Since this integral vanishes, the nontrivial contribution the total impulse comes from evaluating the Coulomb acceleration on the trajectory deflected by the force in (
C.11
) to the desired order. We find

\displaystyle\Delta^{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}=-\pi\frac{\alpha^{4}M^{4}\nu^{2}}{b^{4}}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}\frac{1}{3v_{\infty}}\frac{{\boldsymbol{b}}}{b}\,.

(C.13)

We discover
\Delta^{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}=-\Delta^{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}
, such that the contribution to the scattering angle becomes (using the same convention for a positive angle along the
-\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}
direction)

\chi^{\rm tot}_{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}=\frac{|\Delta^{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}|}{M\nu v_{\infty}}=\pi\frac{\alpha^{4}M^{3}\nu}{3b^{4}v_{\infty}^{2}}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}\,,

(C.14)

which, amusingly, also agrees at leading PN order with the conservative result in
[
82
]
. As we demonstrate next, this is expected due to the nature of the radiation-reaction-square force, and will be recovered as well from the in-out (Feynman) computation, as we demonstrate momentarily.

RR
2
force is conservative

Before proceeding, let us demonstrate that the radiation-reaction-square force in (
C.10
), to the order we are concerned about, is conservative. We already found the conservation of momentum. Let us look now at the loss of energy of each body, say particle 1,

\displaystyle\dot{E}_{1}\equiv m_{1}{\boldsymbol{v}}_{1}\cdot{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1(\textrm{RR}^{2})}=Am_{1}q_{1}\dot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\cdot\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\bigg[m_{1}q_{1}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}+m_{2}q_{2}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}\bigg]\,,

(C.15)

where, in order to simplify notation, we introduced
A\equiv(4/9)\alpha^{2}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)
. After performing the following manipulations

\displaystyle\dot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\cdot\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\cdot\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\Big(\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}^{2}+\cdots\Big)\,,

(C.16)

\displaystyle\dot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\cdot\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\cdot\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,.

(C.17)

we find, up to total time derivatives,

\dot{E}_{1}=-Am_{1}m_{2}q_{1}q_{2}\,\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\cdot\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,.

(C.18)

and similarly,

\dot{E}_{2}=-Am_{2}m_{1}q_{1}q_{2}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}\cdot\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,.

(C.19)

so that

\displaystyle\dot{E}_{1}+\dot{E}_{2}

\displaystyle=-Am_{2}q_{2}m_{1}q_{1}\Big(\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\cdot\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}+\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}\cdot\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\Big)+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)

\displaystyle=-Am_{2}q_{2}m_{1}q_{1}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\Big(\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}\cdot\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}+\cdots\Big)=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,,

(C.20)

becomes a total derivative, which can then be moved to the left-hand side to define a new conserved quantity. Similar steps can be performed for the angular momentum. In that case we have

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{L}}^{i}_{1}

\displaystyle=m_{1}\epsilon^{ijk}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{j}{\boldsymbol{a}}^{k}_{1(\rm RR^{2})}=Am_{1}q_{1}\epsilon^{ijk}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{j}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\bigg[m_{1}q_{1}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{1}+m_{2}q_{2}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{2}\bigg]\,,

(C.21)

and using the identities,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{j}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\dot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}^{j}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{1}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)=\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}^{j}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{1}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,.

(C.22)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{j}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\dot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}^{j}\dddot{x}^{k}_{2}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)=\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}^{j}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{2}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,,

(C.23)

the only nontrivial contribution becomes

\dot{\boldsymbol{L}}^{i}_{1}=Am_{1}q_{1}m_{2}q_{2}\epsilon^{ijk}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}^{j}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{2}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,.

(C.24)

and likewise for particle 2,

\dot{\boldsymbol{L}}^{i}_{2}=Am_{2}q_{2}m_{1}q_{1}\epsilon^{ijk}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}^{j}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{1}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,,

(C.25)

such that, for the total loss of angular momentum,

\dot{\boldsymbol{L}}^{i}_{1}+\dot{\boldsymbol{L}}_{2}^{i}=Am_{2}m_{1}q_{2}q_{1}\epsilon^{ijk}\Big(\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}^{j}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{2}+\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}^{j}\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{k}_{1}\Big)+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}(\cdots)\,,

(C.26)

which, once again, becomes a total derivative.

In-out
computation

We show now how to derive the conservative-like contribution to the scattering angle using the in-out approach. The effective action is obtained by integrating out the electromagnetic field. Following similar steps as before, we find

\displaystyle S^{\rm cons}_{\textrm{(RR)}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}(4\pi\alpha)\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega}{2\pi}\omega^{2}d^{i}(-\omega)d^{i}(\omega)\int_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\frac{1}{(\omega+i0^{+})^{2}-{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}

(C.27)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\alpha\int\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\omega}{2\pi}(i\omega^{3})\textrm{Sign}(\omega)d^{i}(-\omega)d^{i}(\omega)=-i\frac{\alpha}{3\pi}\int{\mathrm{d}}t_{1}{\mathrm{d}}t_{2}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}{\boldsymbol{d}}(t_{1})\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}^{(3)}(t_{2})\,,

The overall factor of
1/2
difference with respect to the in-in result in (
C.4
) is due to the symmetry of the diagram, and in the last step we used (
5.1
). The reader will immediately notice that, provided the time derivatives of the dipole term are real functions of time (as expected from Coulomb-like interactions) the real part of the above in-out effective action vanishes, which is consistent with the fact that there is no conservative contribution at leading order in the radiation-reaction force. (The imaginary part, on the other hand, directly leads to the well-known dipole emission formula:
P\sim{\boldsymbol{d}}^{(2)}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}^{(2)}
, as expected from the optical theorem
[
17
]
.) We can, nonetheless, obtain an acceleration using the generalized Euler-Lagrangian equations
[
16
]
,

{\boldsymbol{F}}_{a}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial{\boldsymbol{x}}_{a}(t)}-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{a}(t)}+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\ddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{a}(t)}-\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{3}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{3}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{a}(t)}+\cdots\,,

(C.28)

and we get

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\rm cons}_{\rm(RR)a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{i\alpha}{3\pi}Q_{a}\int{\mathrm{d}}t_{1}{\mathrm{d}}t_{1}\bigg[\delta(t-t_{1})\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}{\boldsymbol{d}}^{(3)}(t_{2})-{\boldsymbol{d}}(t_{1})\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{3}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{3}}\bigg(\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}\delta(t-t_{2})\bigg)\bigg]\,

(C.29)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2i\alpha}{3\pi}Q_{a}\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t-t^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{d}}^{(3)}(t^{\prime})=-\frac{2i\alpha}{3\pi}m_{a}q_{a}\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t-t^{\prime}}\big(m_{1}q_{1}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}(t^{\prime})+m_{2}q_{2}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}(t^{\prime})\big)\,.

The fact that the leading radiation-reaction force is imaginary is a consequence of the fact that radiative effects dissipative energy at leading order, and therefore, from the decomposition of the retarded Green’s function into Feynman plus a
reactive
term
[
27
]
, only the latter contributes. However, that is no longer the case at second order, as we demonstrate in what follows.

To obtain the radiation-reaction-square effects, we replace the second derivatives in (
C.29
) with the very same acceleration, yielding

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm cons}_{{\rm(RR^{2})}a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{9\pi^{2}}q_{a}\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t-t^{\prime}}\bigg[m_{1}q_{1}^{2}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}}\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime\prime}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime}}\bigg(m_{1}q_{1}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}(t^{\prime\prime})+m_{2}q_{2}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}(t^{\prime\prime})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}}\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime\prime}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime}}\bigg(m_{1}q_{1}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{1}(t^{\prime\prime})+m_{2}q_{2}\dddot{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{2}(t^{\prime\prime})\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{9\pi^{2}}q_{a}(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2})\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t-t^{\prime}}\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime\prime}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime}}\bigg(m_{1}q_{1}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(4)}_{1}(t^{\prime\prime})+m_{2}q_{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(4)}_{2}(t^{\prime\prime})\bigg)\,.

(C.30)

We invoke again the Poincaré-Bertrand theorem
[
90
,
91
]
(see (
5.4
)) and exchange the order of integration, yielding

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm cons}_{{\rm(RR^{2})}a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{9\pi^{2}}q_{a}(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2})\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime\prime}\big(m_{1}q_{1}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(4)}_{1}(t^{\prime\prime})+m_{2}q_{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(4)}_{2}(t^{\prime\prime})\big)\int{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{(t-t^{\prime})(t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle+\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{9}q_{a}(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2})\big(m_{1}q_{1}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(4)}_{1}(t)+m_{2}q_{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(4)}_{2}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{9}q_{a}(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2})\big(m_{1}q_{1}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(4)}_{1}(t)+m_{2}q_{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(4)}_{2}(t)\big)\,,\par

(C.31)

where in the last equality we used the fact that
[
90
,
91
]

\int{\mathrm{d}}t\frac{\mathscr{P}}{(t-t^{\prime})(t^{\prime}-t^{\prime\prime})}=0\,.

(C.32)

We are thus left with the exact same acceleration in (
C.9
) from the in-in approach, which implies that the contributions from the reactive terms all vanish at this order. Following the same steps as before, we get the same value for the scattering angle, consistently with the relativistic result in
[
82
]
.

Radial action

It is instructive to derive the contribution from radiation-reaction-square effects also directly at the level of the (radial) action. This can be done replacing the second derivative in (
C.27
) with the acceleration in (
C.29
), together with (
C.32
), yielding

\displaystyle S_{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}^{\rm cons}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{9\pi^{2}}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)\int{\mathrm{d}}t_{1}{\boldsymbol{d}}(t_{1})\cdot\int{\mathrm{d}}t_{2}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t_{1}-t_{2}}\int{\mathrm{d}}t_{3}\frac{\mathscr{P}}{t_{2}-t_{3}}{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\rm(RR)}^{(4)}(t_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{9}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{d}}(t)\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\rm(RR)}^{(4)}(t)\,,

(C.33)

where

{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\rm(RR)}^{(4)}(t)=\alpha M^{2}\nu q_{1}q_{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}{r^{3}(t)}\right)\,,

(C.34)

such that

S_{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}^{\rm cons}=\frac{2\alpha^{3}}{9}M^{2}\nu\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)q_{1}q_{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})\int{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\boldsymbol{d}}(t)\cdot\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}}{{\mathrm{d}}t^{2}}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{r}}(t)}{r^{3}(t)}\right)\,.

(C.35)

From here we can relate the conservative action, evaluated on the (leading) Coulomb trajectory, to the scattering angle via (see e.g.
[
64
]
)

\chi^{\rm cons}_{\rm(RR^{2})}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial J}S_{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}^{\rm cons}\,,

(C.36)

with
J=M\nu v_{\infty}b
the total angular momentum. Upon inputing the solution for the trajectory, we arrive at

S_{(\textrm{RR}^{2})}^{\rm cons}=\pi\frac{\alpha^{4}M^{4}\nu^{2}}{9b^{3}v_{\infty}}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}\,,

(C.37)

from which we find

\chi^{\rm cons}_{\rm(RR^{2})}=\pi\frac{\alpha^{4}M^{3}\nu}{3b^{4}v_{\infty}^{2}}\big(m_{1}q_{1}^{2}+m_{2}q_{2}^{2}\big)q_{1}^{2}q_{2}^{2}(q_{1}-q_{2})^{2}\,,

(C.38)

in agreement with (
C.14
), which implies as before that
\chi^{\rm tot}_{\rm(RR^{2})}=\chi^{\rm cons}_{\rm(RR^{2})}
at this order.
